+import subprocess
+from pathlib import Path
+from typing import Optional
+from datetime import datetime
+from cstar.execution.handler import ExecutionHandler, ExecutionStatus
+
+
+class LocalProcess(ExecutionHandler):
+    """Execution handler for managing and monitoring local subprocesses.
+
+    This class handles the execution of commands as local subprocesses,
+    allowing users to monitor their status, stream output, and manage
+    lifecycle events such as cancellation.
+
+    Attributes
+    ----------
+    commands : str
+        The shell command(s) to be executed as a subprocess.
+    run_path : Path
+        The directory from which the subprocess will be executed.
+    output_file : Path
+        The file where the subprocess's standard output and error will
+        be written.
+    status : ExecutionStatus
+        The current status of the subprocess, represented as an
+        `ExecutionStatus` enum value.
+
+    Methods
+    -------
+    start()
+        Start the subprocess using the specified command.
+    cancel()
+        Cancel the running subprocess.
+    updates(seconds=10)
+        Stream live updates from the subprocess's output file for a
+        specified duration.
+    """
+
+    def __init__(
+        self,
+        commands: str,
+        output_file: Optional[str | Path] = None,
+        run_path: Optional[str | Path] = None,
+    ):
+        """Initialize a `LocalProcess` instance.
+
+        Parameters
+        ----------
+        commands : str
+            The shell command(s) to execute as a subprocess.
+        output_file : str or Path, optional
+            The file path where the subprocess's standard output and error
+            will be written. Defaults to an auto-generated file in the
+            `run_path` directory.
+        run_path : str or Path, optional
+            The directory from which the subprocess will be executed.
+            Defaults to the current working directory.
+        """
+
+        self.commands = commands
+        self.run_path = Path(run_path) if run_path is not None else Path.cwd()
+        self._default_name = (
+            f"cstar_process_{datetime.strftime(datetime.now(), '%Y%m%d_%H%M%S')}"
+        )
+        self._output_file = (
+            Path(output_file) if output_file is not None else output_file
+        )
+
+        self._output_file_handle = None
+        self._process = None
+        self._cancelled = False
+
+    def start(self):
+        """Start the local process.
+
+        This method initiates the execution of the command specified during
+        initialization. The command runs in a subprocess, with its standard
+        output and error directed to the output file.
+
+        Notes
+        -----
+        - The output file is opened and actively written to during execution.
+        - Use the `status` property to monitor the current state of the
+          subprocess.
+
+        See Also
+        --------
+        cancel : Terminates the running subprocess.
+        """
+
+        # Open the output file to write to
+        self._output_file_handle = open(self.output_file, "w")
+        local_process = subprocess.Popen(
+            self.commands.split(),
+            # shell=True,
+            cwd=self.run_path,
+            stdin=subprocess.PIPE,
+            stdout=self._output_file_handle,
+            stderr=subprocess.STDOUT,
+        )
+        self._process = local_process
+
+    @property
+    def output_file(self) -> Path:
+        """The file in which to write this task's STDOUT and STDERR."""
+        return (
+            self.run_path / f"{self._default_name}.out"
+            if self._output_file is None
+            else self._output_file
+        )
+
+    @property
+    def status(self) -> ExecutionStatus:
+        """Retrieve the current status of the local process.
+
+        This property determines the status of the process based on its
+        lifecycle and return code. The possible statuses are represented by
+        the `ExecutionStatus` enum.
+
+        Returns
+        -------
+        ExecutionStatus
+            The current status of the process. Possible values include:
+            - `ExecutionStatus.UNSUBMITTED`: The task has not been started.
+            - `ExecutionStatus.RUNNING`: The task is currently executing.
+            - `ExecutionStatus.COMPLETED`: The task finished successfully.
+            - `ExecutionStatus.FAILED`: The task finished unsuccessfully.
+            - `ExecutionStatus.CANCELLED`: The task was cancelled using LocalProcess.cancel()
+            - `ExecutionStatus.UNKNOWN`: The task status could not be determined.
+        """
+
+        if self._cancelled:
+            return ExecutionStatus.CANCELLED
+        if self._process is None:
+            return ExecutionStatus.UNSUBMITTED
+        if self._process.poll() is None:
+            return ExecutionStatus.RUNNING
+        if self._process.returncode == 0:
+            if self._output_file_handle:
+                self._output_file_handle.close()
+                self._output_file_handle = None
+            return ExecutionStatus.COMPLETED
+        elif self._process.returncode is not None:
+            if self._output_file_handle:
+                self._output_file_handle.close()
+                self._output_file_handle = None
+            return ExecutionStatus.FAILED
+        return ExecutionStatus.UNKNOWN
+
+    def cancel(self):
+        """Cancel the local process.
+
+        This method terminates the subprocess if it is currently running.
+        A graceful shutdown is attempted using `terminate` (SIGTERM). If the
+        subprocess does not terminate within a timeout period, it is forcefully
+        killed using `kill` (SIGKILL).
+
+        Notes
+        -----
+        - The status of the subprocess is updated to `ExecutionStatus.CANCELLED`
+          after termination or killing.
+        - The method ensures that the output file handle is closed after
+          cancelling the process.
+
+        See Also
+        --------
+        wait : Wait for the local process to finish
+        """
+
+        if self._process and self.status == ExecutionStatus.RUNNING:
+            try:
+                self._process.terminate()  # Send SIGTERM to allow graceful shutdown
+                self._process.wait(timeout=5)  # Wait for it to terminate
+            except subprocess.TimeoutExpired:
+                self._process.kill()  # Forcefully kill if it doesn't terminate
+            finally:
+                if self._output_file_handle:
+                    self._output_file_handle.close()
+                    self._output_file_handle = None
+                self._cancelled = True
+        else:
+            print(f"Cannot cancel job with status '{self.status}'")
+            return
+
+    def wait(self):
+        """Wait for the local process to finish.
+
+        See Also
+        --------
+        cancel : end the current process
+        """
+
+        if self._process and self.status == ExecutionStatus.RUNNING:
+            self._process.wait()
+        else:
+            print(f"cannot wait for process with execution status '{self.status}'")

## Teach Fractions Through Word Problems

Teaching fractions can often seem like a daunting task. You may hear many the groan or sigh when you open a book to the section on fractions. This does not have to be the case. In fact, most students will not dread a topic once they feel confident working with the concept.

The concept of a “fraction”<#sep#>-

#include <iostream>
#include <vector>
#include <cmath>
#include <iomanip>

using namespace std;

struct Point {
    double x, y, z;
    Point(double x = 0, double y = 0, double z = 0) : x(x), y(y), z(z) {}
};

int main() {
    int n;
    cin >> n;
    vector<Point> points;
    for (int i = 0; i <= n; ++i) {
        double x, y, z;
        cin >> x >> y >> z;
        points.emplace_back(x, y, z);
    }
    double vp, vs;
    cin >> vp >> vs;
    Point P;
    cin >> P.x >> P.y >> P.z;

    double earliest_time = 1e18;
    Point best_point;

    double T_prev = 0.0;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        const Point& p_i = points[i];
        const Point& p_next = points[i+1];
        double dx = p_next.x - p_i.x;
        double dy = p_next.y - p_i.y;
        double dz = p_next.z - p_i.z;
        double len_i = sqrt(dx*dx + dy*dy + dz*dz);
        if (len_i < 1e-9) continue;

        double dt_i = len_i / vs;
        double t_start = T_prev;
        double t_end = t_start + dt_i;

        double a = vs*vs - vp*vp;

        double x_diff = p_i.x - P.x;
        double y_diff = p_i.y - P.y;
        double z_diff = p_i.z - P.z;

        double dx_dt = dx * vs / len_i;
        double dy_dt = dy * vs / len_i;
        double dz_dt = dz * vs / len_i;

        double sum_part = x_diff * dx_dt + y_diff * dy_dt + z_diff * dz_dt;
        double b_part = sum_part - vp*vp * t_start;
        double b = 2 * b_part;

        double c = x_diff*x_diff + y_diff*y_diff + z_diff*z_diff - (vp * t_start) * (vp * t_start);

        vector<double> solutions;

        if (a == 0) {
            if (b == 0) {
                if (c == 0) {
                    solutions.push_back(0.0);
                }
            } else {
                double s = -c / b;
                if (s >= -1e-9 && s <= dt_i + 1e-9) {
                    solutions.push_back(s);
                }
            }
        } else {
            double D = b*b - 4*a*c;
            if (D >= -1e-9) {
                D = max(D, 0.0);
                double sqrtD = sqrt(D);
                double s1 = (-b + sqrtD) / (2*a);
                double s2 = (-b - sqrtD) / (2*a);
                for (double s : {s1, s2}) {
                    if (s >= -1e-9 && s <= dt_i + 1e-9) {
                        solutions.push_back(s);
                    }
                }
            }
        }

        for (double s : solutions) {
            s = max(0.0, min(s, dt_i));
            double t = t_start + s;
            double ratio = s / dt_i;
            double x = p_i.x + dx * ratio;
            double y = p_i.y + dy * ratio;
            double z = p_i.z + dz * ratio;
            double dist = sqrt((x-P.x)*(x-P.x) + (y-P.y)*(y-P.y) + (z-P.z)*(z-P.z));
            if (abs(dist - vp * t) < 1e-6 && t < earliest_time) {
                earliest_time = t;
                best_point = Point(x, y, z);
            }
        }

        T_prev += dt_i;
    }

    if (earliest_time < 1e18) {
        cout << "YES" << endl;
        cout << fixed << setprecision(10) << earliest_time << endl;
        cout << fixed << setprecision(10) << best_point.x << " " << best_point.y << " " << best_point.z << endl;
    } else {
        cout << "NO" << endl;
    }

    return 0;
}<#sep#> of length r√2. If we set the edge length to 1, then r = 1/√2. The midpoints are at distance r/√2 from the center, so (1/√2)/√2 = 1/2. So distance from center to midpoints is 0.5. Therefore, the distance between a midpoint and a vertex would be sqrt( (r - r/2)^2 + (r/2)^2 )? Wait, between (r,0) and (r/2, r/2):

distance squared is (r - r/2)^2 + (0 - r/2)^2 = (r/2)^2 + ( - r/2)^2 = r²/4 + r²/4 = r²/2. So distance is r / sqrt(2). Since r=1/√2, then the distance is (1/√2)/sqrt(2)=1/2. Which is too small, only 0.5, which is less than 1, so that would violate the distance requirement. So this configuration is bad.

Therefore, arranging a square with midpoints included causes some points to be too close. So this won't work. Thus, going back to the regular octagon with a center is better.

Alternatively, let me consider an equilateral triangle with points on each vertex, centroid, and midpoints. For an equilateral triangle with side length s, the midpoints of the sides are at a distance of (s/2)/sin(60°)= ?

Wait, the centroid is at a distance of (2/3)*height from each vertex. The height of an equilateral triangle is (sqrt(3)/2)s. So the centroid is at (2/3)(sqrt(3)/2 s) = (sqrt(3)/3)s from each vertex.

If we want to have points at the three vertices, three midpoints, and the centroid. Let's see the distances.

Between vertices: the side length s must be at least 1. Between a vertex and a midpoint: the distance is (distance from vertex to midpoint), which is (s/2)/sin(30°)? Wait, the midpoint is halfway along the edge, so the distance from vertex to midpoint is s/2 along the edge. Wait, but the Euclidean distance between a vertex and the midpoint of a side is sqrt( (s/2)^2 + ( (sqrt(3)/2 s - (sqrt(3)/6 s ))^2 ))? Wait, maybe better to calculate coordinates.

Let me place an equilateral triangle with vertices at (0,0), (s,0), and (s/2, (sqrt(3)/2)s). The midpoints of the sides are at (s/2,0), ( (3s/4), (sqrt(3)/4 s)), and (s/4, (sqrt(3)/4 s)). The centroid is at (s/2, (sqrt(3)/6)s).

Calculating the distance from a vertex to a midpoint: Take (0,0) to (s/2,0): distance s/2, which needs to be at least 1. So s must be at least 2. But then the distance between vertices would be s=2, so that's okay.

The distance from vertex to centroid: distance from (0,0) to (s/2, (sqrt(3)/6)s). Let me compute:

sqrt( (s/2)^2 + (sqrt(3)/6 s)^2 ) = s sqrt( 1/4 + (1/12) ) = s sqrt( (3/12 + 1/12 )) = s sqrt(4/12)=s sqrt(1/3)=s/(√3). If s=2, that distance is 2/√3≈1.154, which is more than 1, okay.

Distance between midpoints: for example between (s/2,0) and (s/4, (sqrt(3)/4)s):

distance squared = (s/4)^2 + ( (sqrt(3)/4 s)^2 )= s²/16 + (3 s²)/16 = (4 s²)/16 = s²/4. So distance is s/2. If s=2, that's 1, which is okay.

Distance between centroid and midpoints: from centroid (s/2, (sqrt(3)/6)s) to midpoint (s/2,0):

distance is sqrt( (0)^2 + (sqrt(3)/6 s)^2 ) = sqrt( ( (sqrt(3)/6 s)^2 ))= sqrt(3)/6 s. If s=2, then that distance is sqrt(3)/3 ≈0.577, which is less than 1. That's a problem because the centroid must be at least 1 away from all points. So this configuration doesn't work unless we make s larger. Wait, but if the centroid is too close to the midpoints, then if we set s such that that distance is at least 1.

sqrt(3)/6 s ≥1 → s ≥6 / sqrt(3)=2 sqrt(3)≈3.464. Then, the edge length is 2 sqrt(3), so the vertices are 2 sqrt(3) apart. Then the enclosing circle would have a radius equal to the distance from center to vertex: sqrt(3)/3 * s = sqrt(3)/3 * 2 sqrt(3)=2*3/3=2. So radius 2. Which is worse than the octagon plus center's ~1.306. So this is not good. So probably not the right approach.

Hmm, maybe going back. The octagon plus center seems better. Let me confirm the distances again in that configuration.

Regular octagon with radius R≈1.306, with a point at the center. The distance from the center to any vertex is R≈1.306, which is more than 1, good. The edges between the octagon vertices are length 1, since that was our starting assumption (edge length=1). The distance between the center and any other point is okay, and all other points are vertices of the octagon which have their distances at least 1 between each other.

Additionally, between a vertex and another vertex two steps apart (i.e., separated by one vertex), what is their distance? The central angle between them would be 2*(45°)=90°, so chord length is 2R sin(45°)=2 R*(√2/2)= R*√2≈1.306*1.414≈1.847>1. So all distances are okay.

Thus, this configuration satisfies the problem's conditions with R≈1.306. Is there a way to make R smaller?

Suppose we arrange the points differently. For instance, can we place 9 points in a different symmetric shape with a smaller R?

Another thought: what if instead of an octagon plus center, we use a different polygon with more sides? Wait, a nonagon would have smaller radius, but as we saw before, a regular nonagon with side length 1 gives a larger radius (~1.46). So that's worse.

Alternatively, using a square grid with a smarter arrangement?

Suppose arranging 8 points on the vertices of a square, but with a smaller square and adding a center point. Wait, but to have the minimum distance between points as 1, the square must be at least 1x1. So side length 1, then the diagonal is sqrt(2), so the radius from center would be sqrt(0.5² +0.5²)=sqrt(0.5)=≈0.707, but then the center is at distance 0.707 from the edges, but distance from center to vertices is sqrt(0.5)≈0.707, so the center would only be 0.707 away from the vertices, which is less than 1, so that's no good. Hence the 3x3 grid with spacing 1 is required for the square.

Alternatively, maybe an irregular polygon or another configuration. Maybe points arranged in three dimensions? Wait, no, the problem specifies points on a plane.

Alternatively, arrange points in a 3-layered structure. Let me think of a hexagon with three points on each layer. Hmm, but getting confused.

Alternatively, maybe it's known that the minimal enclosing circle for 9 points with mutual distance 1 is indeed the octagon plus center case. Therefore, the minimal R is 1/(2 sin(π/8)), which is exactly 1/(2 sin 22.5°).

To write this in a more simplified form, using exact trigonometric values:

sin(22.5°) = sin(π/8) = √(2 - √2)/2. Hence,

R = 1 / (2 * [√(2 - √2<#sep#> be written as

## Teach Fractions Through Word Problems

• Math Tutorials
• Pre Algebra & Algebra
• Exponential Decay

Teaching fractions can often seem like a daunting task. You may hear many the groan or sigh when you open a book to the section on fractions. This does not have to be the case. In fact, most students will not dread a topic once they feel confident working with the concept.

The concept of a “fraction” is abstract. Visualizing apart versus a whole is a developmental skill not fully grasped by some students until middle or high school. There are a few ways to get your class embracing fractions, and there are a number of worksheets you can print out to nail the concept home for your students.

## Make Fractions Relatable

Children, in fact, students of all ages prefer a hands-on demonstration or an interactive experience to pencil-and-paper math equations. You can get felt circles to make pie graphs, you can play with fraction dice, or even use a set of dominoes to help explain the concept of fractions.

If you can, order an actual pizza. Or, if you happen to celebrate a class birthday, well perhaps make it a "fraction" birthday cake. When you engage the senses, you have a higher engagement of the audience. Also, the lesson has a great chance of permanence, too.

You can print fraction circles so your students can illustrate fractions as they learn. Have them touch the felt circles, let them watch you create a felt circle pie representing a fraction, ask your class to color in the corresponding fraction circle. Then, ask your class to write the fraction out.

## Have Fun with Math

As we all know, not every student learns the same way. Some children are better at visual processing than auditory processing. Others prefer tactile learning with hand-held manipulatives or may prefer games.

Games make what could be a dry and boring topic more fun and interesting. They provide that visual component that might make all the difference.

There are plenty of online teaching tools with challenges for your students to use. Let them practice digitally. Online resources can help solidify concepts.

## Fraction Word Problems

A problem is, by definition, a situation that causes perplexity. A primary tenet of teaching through problem-solving is that students confronted with real-life problems are forced into a state of needing to connect what they know with the problem at hand. Learning through problem-solving develops understanding.

A student's mental capacity grows more complex with time. Solving problems can force them to think deeply and to connect, extend, and elaborate on their prior knowledge.

## Common Pitfall

Sometimes you can spend too much time teaching fraction concepts, like "simplify," "find the common denominators," "use the four operations," that we often forget the value of word problems. Encourage students to apply their knowledge of fraction concepts through problem-solving and word problems.

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);
    
    int n, m;
    cin >> n >> m;
    
    vector<vector<int>> words(n);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        int l;
        cin >> l;
        words[i].resize(l);
        for (int j = 0; j < l; ++j) {
            cin >> words[i][j];
        }
    }
    
    vector<bool> must_include(m + 1, false);
    vector<bool> must_exclude(m + 1, false);
    vector<vector<int>> adj(m + 1);
    
    for (int i = 0; i < n - 1; ++i) {
        const auto& a = words[i];
        const auto& b = words[i + 1];
        
        bool is_prefix = true;
        int min_len = min(a.size(), b.size());
        int j;
        for (j = 0; j < min_len; ++j) {
            if (a[j] != b[j]) {
                is_prefix = false;
                break;
            }
        }
        if (is_prefix) {
            if (a.size() > b.size()) {
                cout << "No\n";
                return 0;
            }
            continue;
        }
        
        int x = a[j];
        int y = b[j];
        if (x > y) {
            must_include[x] = true;
            must_exclude[y] = true;
        } else if (x < y) {
            adj[y].push_back(x);
        }
    }
    
    for (int i = 1; i <= m; ++i) {
        if (must_include[i] && must_exclude[i]) {
            cout << "No\n";
            return 0;
        }
    }
    
    vector<bool> in_S(m + 1, false);
    queue<int> q;
    
    for (int i = 1; i <= m; ++i) {
        if (must_include[i]) {
            if (must_exclude[i]) {
                cout << "No\n";
                return 0;
            }
            if (!in_S[i]) {
                in_S[i] = true;
                q.push(i);
            }
        }
    }
    
    while (!q.empty()) {
        int u = q.front();
        q.pop();
        
        for (int x : adj[u]) {
            if (must_exclude[x]) {
                cout << "No\n";
                return 0;
            }
            if (!in_S[x]) {
                in_S[x] = true;
                q.push(x);
            }
        }
    }
    
    for (int i = 1; i <= m; ++i) {
        if (must_exclude[i] && in_S[i]) {
            cout << "No\n";
            return 0;
        }
    }
    
    vector<int> result;
    for (int i = 1; i <= m; ++i) {
        if (in_S[i]) {
            result.push_back(i);
        }
    }
    
    cout << "Yes\n";
    cout << result.size() << '\n';
    for (int x : result) {
        cout << x << ' ';
    }
    cout << '\n';
    
    return 0;

Alternatively, maybe it's known that the minimal enclosing circle for 9 points with mutual distance 1 is indeed the octagon plus center case. Therefore, the minimal R is 1/(2 sin(π/8)), which is exactly 1/(2 sin 22.5°).

To write this in a more simplified form, using exact trigonometric values:

sin(22.5°) = sin(π/8) = √(2 - √2)/2. Hence,

R = 1 / (2 * [√(2 - √2)/2]) = 1 / √(2 - √2).

Rationalizing the denominator:

Multiply numerator and denominator by √(2 + √2):

R = √(2 + √2) / √( (2 - √2)(2 + √2) ) = √(2 + √2)/√(4 - 2) = √(2 + √2)/√2 = √( (2 + √2)/2 ).

Simplify:

√( (2 + √2)/2 ) = √(1 + (√2)/2 ). But perhaps the first form is acceptable. Anyway, numerically, that's approximately 1.306.

Is there a configuration that can achieve a smaller radius?

Let me consider another approach: place 8 points on a circle of radius r, forming a regular octagon with each edge length d, and a ninth point at the center. The minimal distance between any two points must be at least 1. The distance between the center and any vertex is r. The distance between adjacent vertices is d=2r sin(π/8), which must be at least 1. The distance between the center and any vertex is r which must be at least 1. So we have two constraints:

1. d =2r sin(π/8) ≥1

2. r ≥1

But to minimize R, which is the radius of the enclosing circle, we want to set r as small as possible. So we set r equal to the maximum of (1/(2 sin(π/8)), 1). Since 1/(2 sin(π/8))≈1.306>1, so the first constraint is the binding one. Thus, the minimal r required is exactly 1/(2 sin(π/8)). So that configuration is indeed the minimal one.

Alternatively, can we place more points near the center without violating the minimal distance? If we add a second central point, it would need to be at least 1 away from all vertices and from the first central point, but the distance from the center to vertices is r=1.306, so placing two points at the center would require them to be at least 1 apart, so they can't both be exactly at the center. So instead, maybe slightly offset?

Suppose moving the two center points apart from each other. Let's see, if we have two points near the center, each must be at least 1 away from each other and from all vertices. The distance from each to the vertices must be at least 1, so they have to be within a circle of radius r (distance to vertices is r, so moving them inward would bring them closer to vertices). Wait, actually if they are placed inside, their distance to the vertices would be larger than r? Wait, no, the distance from an inner point to a vertex depends on direction. For example, if you place a point near the center but closer to a vertex, the distance to that vertex would be less.

This complicates things. Perhaps having two central points would require moving all the outer points further out to maintain their distance to the inner points, which might result in a larger enclosing circle. Hence, the configuration with one center point and eight on the octagon might be better.

## What can QuickMath do?

QuickMath will automatically answer the most common problems in algebra, equations and calculus faced by high-school and college students.

• The algebra section allows you to expand, factor or simplify virtually any expression you choose. It also has commands for splitting fractions into partial fractions, combining several fractions into one and cancelling common factors within a fraction.
• The equations section lets you solve an equation or system of equations. You can usually find the exact answer or, if necessary, a numerical answer to almost any accuracy you require.
• The inequalities section lets you solve an inequality or a system of inequalities for a single variable. You can also plot inequalities in two variables.
• The calculus section will carry out differentiation as well as definite and indefinite integration.
• The matrices section contains commands for the arithmetic manipulation of matrices.
• The graphs section contains commands for plotting equations and inequalities.
• The numbers section has a percentages command for explaining the most common types of percentage problems and a section for dealing with scientific notation.

## Homework help

The downsides of free homework help..

First, and the main downside is the inability to check the result. People helping you with your task might not know the answers themselves. And what assistance would you get in this case? Wrong answers!

Second, it's the proficiency of the strangers on the other side of the screen. Who are they? College professors who came to provide homework help for free? Or just students like you who might not be able to cope with a simple task. They might not now anything about the subject, or might be way too self-confident and give you wrong answers.

Think twice before using the help from these people. In the next part of the article we describe the most popular sources of homework assistance and give our honest opinion about them.

## The best websites for homework help.

When a student is looking for help he always (no exaggeration!) expects it to be free. Of course, being a college student always means being tight on budget and looking for the ways to save money even more. But it should be related to college homework help. Sadly, there are thousands of examples when a bad homework resulted in bad grades, and a poor student had to learn more to pass the exam. That's why we don't recommend to look for answers on the websites like Reddit or Chegg. Why? We explain below.

Chegg homework help is a paid service. You need to buy a monthly subscription to use it. Is it worth it? Depends on your professor. If the tasks you get are strictly from a textbook, then it might be good. To cope with a creative teachers this website has nothing to offer.

The same goes for Reddit homework help. It might be useful when you're looking for solutions on a standard task, the one that dozens of people are struggling with as well. There's nothing these services can offer if you're homework is unique and created by a teacher himself. What to do in this case? Read our recommendations below.

## We recommend to try it!

There's an easy and cheap solution that will help you succeed in your studies. A personal assistance with homework created just for your tasks. No need to scroll pages looking for similar tasks and<#sep#>-

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

const int MOD = 1e9 + 7;
int n, m, k;

struct ACNode {
    array<int, 20> trans;
    int fail;
    int value;
    ACNode() : fail(0), value(0) { trans.fill(-1); }
};

vector<ACNode> trie;

void insert(const vector<int>& s, int val) {
    int u = 0;
    for (int c : s) {
        if (trie[u].trans[c] == -1) {
            trie[u].trans[c] = trie.size();
            trie.emplace_back();
        }
        u = trie[u].trans[c];
    }
    trie[u].value += val;
}

void build_automaton() {
    queue<int> q;
    for (int c = 0; c < m; ++c) {
        if (trie[0].trans[c] == -1) {
            trie[0].trans[c] = 0;
        } else {
            q.push(trie[0].trans[c]);
            trie[trie[0].trans[c]].fail = 0;
        }
    }
    while (!q.empty()) {
        int u = q.front(); q.pop();
        for (int c = 0; c < m; ++c) {
            if (trie[u].trans[c] != -1) {
                int v = trie[u].trans[c];
                int f = trie[u].fail;
                while (f != 0 && trie[f].trans[c] == -1) f = trie[f].fail;
                trie[v].fail = trie[f].trans[c];
                trie[v].value += trie[trie[v].fail].value;
                q.push(v);
            } else {
                trie[u].trans[c] = trie[trie[u].fail].trans[c];
            }
        }
    }
}

vector<int> subtract_one(vector<int> digits) {
    if (digits.empty()) return {};
    int i = digits.size() - 1;
    while (i >= 0 && digits[i] == 0) digits[i--] = m - 1;
    if (i < 0) return {};
    digits[i]--;
    int first_non_zero = 0;
    while (first_non_zero < digits.size() && digits[first_non_zero] == 0) first_non_zero++;
    if (first_non_zero >= digits.size()) return {};
    digits = vector<int>(digits.begin() + first_non_zero, digits.end());
    return digits;
}

int count(const vector<int>& digits) {
    if (digits.empty()) return 0;
    int len = digits.size();
    vector<vector<vector<vector<vector<int>>>>> memo(len, 
        vector<vector<vector<vector<int>>>>(2,
            vector<vector<vector<int>>>(trie.size(),
                vector<vector<int>>(k+1, vector<int>(2, -1)))));
    function<int(int, bool, int, int, bool)> dp = [&](int pos, bool tight, int state, int sum_so_far, bool started) {
        if (pos == len) return started && sum_so_far <= k ? 1 : 0;
        if (memo[pos][tight][state][sum_so_far][started] != -1) return memo[pos][tight][state][sum_so_far][started];
        int limit = tight ? digits[pos] : m - 1;
        int res = 0;
        for (int d = 0; d <= limit; ++d) {
            bool new_tight = tight && (d == limit);
            bool new_started = started;
            int new_state = state;
            int new_sum = sum_so_far;
            if (!started) {
                if (d == 0) {
                    new_state = 0;
                } else {
                    new_started = true;
                    new_state = trie[0].trans[d];
                    new_sum += trie[new_state].value;
                    if (new_sum > k) continue;
                }
            } else {
                new_state = trie[state].trans[d];
                new_sum += trie[new_state].value;
                if (new_sum > k) continue;
            }
            res = (res + dp(pos + 1, new_tight, new_state, new_sum, new_started)) % MOD;
        }
        return memo[pos][tight][state][sum_so_far][started] = res;
    };
    return dp(0, true, 0, 0, false);
}

int main() {
    ios::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);
    trie.emplace_back();
    cin >> n >> m >> k;
    auto read_digits = [](int& len) {
        cin >> len;
        vector<int> res(len);
        for (int i = 0; i < len; ++i) cin >> res[i];
        return res;
    };
    vector<int> l_digits = read_digits(n), r_digits = read_digits(n);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        auto s = read_digits(n);
        int v; cin >> v;
        insert(s, v);
    }
    build_automaton();
    vector<int> l_minus_1 = subtract_one(l_digits);
    int A = count(r_digits);
    int B = l_minus_1.empty() ? 0 : count(l_minus_1);
    cout << (A - B + MOD) % MOD << endl;
    return 0;

Let me place one new point between two existing outer points, say between the points at 0° and 60°. The two original points are separated by 60°, so the midpoint in angle is 30°. The distance between the new point at (R, 30°) and the original points at (1,0°) and (1,60°) should be at least 1.

Calculating the distance from (R,30°) to (1,0°):

Using the law of cosines: sqrt( R² + 1² - 2*R*1*cos(30°) ) ≥1.

Similarly to (1,60°):

sqrt( R² +1 - 2R cos(30°) ) ≥1.

Set the first equation:

sqrt(R² +1 - 2R*(√3/2)) ≥1.

Square both sides:

R² +1 - 2R*(√3/2) ≥1 → R² - √3 R ≥0 → R(R - √3) ≥0. Thus, R ≥√3≈1.732.

Similarly, the two new points need to be at least 1 apart from each other. If placed symmetrically at angles 30° and 330°, their angular separation is 60°, distance between them is sqrt(R² + R² - 2R² cos(60°)) = sqrt(2R² - R²) = R. So this distance must be at least 1, so R≥1, which is already satisfied. But if R=√3, then their distance is √3≈1.732≥1, which is okay.

So adding two points at radius √3 and angles 30° and 330° gives 9 points. But the total radius required is R=√3≈1.732. Which is worse than the octagon plus center case. Thus, not better.

Alternatively, if we place the two new points further apart?

Alternatively, maybe arrange both new points on the opposite side? Not sure. Anyway, the required R here is higher than the octagon configuration, so it's worse.

Another approach: perhaps arrange points in three rows, like a hexagon but with two rows?

Wait, arranging in a triangular lattice. Let me think of a hexagonal grid with three layers. The central point, and then layers around it.

In a hexagonal packing, the first ring around the center has 6 points, second ring has 12, etc. But to get 9 points, we can have 1 + 6 + 2 =9, but those 2 would have to be placed in the second ring but that might not maintain distances.

Alternatively, let's try a hexagonal packing with a central point, first ring 6 points, and then two more points on the second ring. But the second ring points are further out, but their distance to first ring points must be at least 1. The distance between first and second ring points in a hex grid is sqrt(3) times the lattice spacing. Let me think in terms of side length.

Suppose the first ring is at distance 1 from the center (so the first layer is radius 1). The distance between adjacent points in the first ring is 1, forming a hexagon. The second ring would be at a distance of 2 from the center, but the distance between second ring points and first ring points would be sqrt(3) ~1.732. But if we only place two points in the second ring, spaced such that they are 1 unit away from some first ring points and each other?

This is getting too vague. Let me think of actual coordinates.

Let me suppose the center at (0,0). First ring six points at (1,0), (0.5, sqrt(3)/2), (-0.5, sqrt(3)/2), (-1,0), (-0.5, -sqrt(3)/2), (0.5, -sqrt(3)/2). This forms a regular hexagon with side length 1. Adding two more points. The second ring points would normally be at distance 2, but adding two there might not satisfy minimal distance. Alternatively, place them at the same distance as the first ring?

Wait, but inserting two points into the first ring would require angles 30° and 330°, but they would be too close to existing points. For instance, placing a point at (cos(30°), sin(30°)) = (sqrt(3)/2, 0.5). The distance to (1,0) would be sqrt( (sqrt(3)/2 -1)^2 + (0.5)^2 ) = sqrt( (approx 0.866/2 -1?) Wait exact calculation:

Let me compute the distance between (1,0) and (cos(30°), sin(30°)) = (sqrt(3)/2, 1/2):

dx = sqrt(3)/2 -1, dy =1/2 -0.

dx squared: (sqrt(3)/2 -1)^2 = ( (sqrt(3) - 2)/2 )² = ( (sqrt(3)-2)^2 )/4.

dy squared: (1/2)^2 =1/4.

Total distance squared: [ (sqrt(3)-2)^2 +1 ] /4.

Expand (sqrt(3)-2)^2 =3 -4 sqrt(3) +4 =7 -4 sqrt(3).

Thus total distance squared: (7 -4 sqrt(3) +1)/4? Wait no: wait no, wait:

Wait, (sqrt(3)-2)^2 = (sqrt(3))^2 -4 sqrt(3) +4 =3 -4 sqrt(3)+4 =7 -4 sqrt(3).

Adding 1 to that? Wait, no:

Wait the total distance squared is [ (sqrt(3)-2)^2 +1 ] /4?

Wait original expression:

dx squared + dy squared = [ (sqrt(3)/2 -1)^2 + (1/2)^2 ].

Wait let me recompute:

dx = sqrt(3)/2 -1 ≈0.866 -1≈-0.134

dx² ≈0.018.

dy =1/2 -0=0.5, dy²=0.25.

Total ≈0.018+0.25=0.268. So distance ≈sqrt(0.268)≈0.517 <1. So too small. Thus inserting points in between existing hexagon points results in distances too small.

Thus that approach won't work.

Given all this, I think the best configuration found so far is the regular octagon with eight vertices and a center point, requiring R = 1/(2 sin(π/8)) ≈1.306.

Is this the known minimal? Let me think if there's another configuration with 9 points.

Wait, what about points arranged as a 3x3 grid but with the points shifted or scaled?

Suppose instead of a square grid, a square grid rotated and scaled such that the diagonal distances are 1, but that might not work.

Suppose the 3x3 grid has horizontal and vertical spacing of s instead of 1. The diagonal distance between adjacent points (like from (0,0) to (1,1)) would be s√2. To have the minimal distance between points be 1, either the grid spacing s or the diagonal s√2 must be at least 1. To minimize the radius, we can set the minimal distance between any two adjacent points (horizontally/vertically) as 1, so s=1, which brings us back to the original square grid with radius sqrt(2) ≈1.414.

Alternatively, set the diagonal distance to 1, so s√2 =1 → s=1/√2≈0.707. Then, the horizontal/vertical distance would be 0.707, which is less than 1, violating the minimal distance. So that won't work.

Alternatively, some other scaling?

Suppose the minimal distance is 1 between all points, so both the grid spacing s and the diagonal distances must be ≥1. Thus s must be at least 1, so the grid spacing must be 1. Hence the radius remains sqrt(2).

Hence, the square grid cannot be improved in that way.

Given all that, I think the octagon with center configuration is better. Therefore, I believe the minimal R is 1 divided by (2 times sine of 22.5 degrees).
